So… I don’t know if I’d say it’s “just a worry.” It feels like something I can see, even if other people say they can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your face being uneven or puffy might not be entirely accurate?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s a possibility. I don’t know how big of a possibility it feels like in the moment, if that makes sense. When I’m actually looking at myself on a video call, or I see a salsa photo where the angle catches the left side of my face, it feels very obvious to me—like the unevenness and puffiness are right there, and I can’t understand how someone wouldn’t notice it.\n\nBut then, outside of that moment, when I’m talking about it like this, I can admit that maybe my perception isn’t completely accurate. Because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see what I’m seeing, or that it’s not noticeable, and I guess it would be strange if literally no one else could see something that feels so glaring to me. So part of me knows I might be zeroing in on it in a way that makes it look worse to me than it really is.\n\nIt’s just… that doesn’t stop me from focusing on it. That’s why I came in, really. I can’t stop checking or noticing how uneven and puffy the left side looks to me, especially on work video calls and in photos from salsa, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life. So yes, I can say there’s a possibility I’m not seeing it accurately, but emotionally it still feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal to others, even if it doesn't to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s possible. I mean, I can say that sitting here, because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything wrong, or they’ll say I look normal, and logically I know that probably means it might look normal to other people.\n\nBut when I’m actually seeing myself, especially on video calls for work, it’s like my eyes go straight to the left side of my face. It looks uneven to me, kind of puffy, and then I start thinking, “How could nobody else be noticing this?” And the same thing happens with salsa photos. I’ll see a picture and I can barely look at the rest of it because I’m focused on that side of my face.\n\nSo yes, I guess it could look normal to other people even if it doesn’t look normal to me. That’s part of why I came in, honestly, because I can’t stop focusing on it even with people reassuring me, and it’s starting to affect work calls and whether I want to go out socially. It just doesn’t feel normal to me when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face might be different from how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it might be different. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to say that because when I’m looking at myself, it really doesn’t feel like “just my view.” It feels like I’m seeing something obvious—the left side of my face looks uneven, kind of puffy, especially on video calls or in those salsa photos. My attention just locks onto it.\n\nBut then I also know I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and that probably says something. On work video calls I’m distracted by it, and socially, with photos from dancing or just being around people, I keep worrying about how it looks. So yes, I think my view could be different from reality, but in the moment it feels very real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being uneven or puffy is based on something you actually see, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on something I actually see, or at least I feel like I’m seeing it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… definitely. I mean, I don’t think of it as a “ritual” when I’m doing it, but it probably is, because it’s this whole sequence I get stuck in.

Before a work meeting, especially if it’s on video, I’ll open the camera preview over and over. I’ll check how the left side of my face looks, then I’ll shift my laptop a little, change the angle, turn a lamp on or off, move closer to the window, then decide the lighting makes it look worse, then move again. And I’ll keep adjusting my hair around that side, like trying to cover it or soften it somehow, even if it probably just looks like I’m fussing with my hair.

It happens before going out too. If I’m meeting friends or going dancing, I’ll take pictures to see how it looks, but then I don’t trust the first picture, so I take another one from a different angle, then another. Sometimes I retake them so many times that I feel worse than when I started, because I’m just staring at that side of my face.

And I do ask people. Mostly friends. I’ll ask, “Does my face look weird?” or “Does this side look puffy?” And even if they say no, I’ll ask again in a different way, like maybe they didn’t understand what I meant. I know it probably gets annoying. It’s embarrassing to admit that.

The worst part is that it makes me late. I’ll think I’m just checking quickly before a meeting or before leaving, but then twenty or thirty minutes can go by, and by the end I’m either rushing and upset, or I feel so drained that I don’t even want to go anymore.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main reason I came in is that I can’t stop focusing on my face, specifically the left side. It looks uneven to me, and kind of puffy, especially on video calls for work or in photos from salsa nights. I know that probably sounds really specific, but once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nPeople keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that I look normal, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I keep checking how I look on camera or in pictures, and then I get distracted and self-conscious. It’s starting to affect work because I’m on video calls a lot, and socially it’s made salsa less fun because I’m worried about how I’ll look in photos afterward. So… yeah, it’s gotten to the point where it’s interfering with things, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking or fixing, because of how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. It’s kind of embarrassing how much time it takes up.\n\nBefore a work meeting, especially if it’s on video, I’ll open the camera preview over and over just to see how my face looks from that angle. I’ll move my laptop up, then down, then turn slightly, then change the lighting, and I’ll keep checking whether the left side looks swollen or uneven. Sometimes I’ll fix my hair so it covers that side a little, then I’ll decide that looks weird too, so I’ll redo it. It can become this whole routine where I’m not even preparing for the meeting anymore, I’m just trying to make my face look less noticeable.\n\nAnd if I’m going out, it’s similar. I’ll take pictures of myself, delete them, retake them, change the lighting, retake them again. I’ll ask friends, like, “Do I look strange?” or “Does this side of my face look bigger?” And they’ll say no, but then I’ll ask again in a different way, which I know is probably annoying.\n\nSometimes I end up running late because I can’t stop adjusting things. Or I’ll get so drained from checking and fixing that I don’t even feel like going anymore.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I feel like it should, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just like, “Oh, I don’t like that photo,” and then I move on. It kind of grabs my whole attention. If I’m on a video call and I notice the left side of my face looking puffy or uneven, I can feel myself getting tense and distracted, like I’m trying to listen but part of my brain is just staring at myself in the little camera box. And then afterward I’ll keep thinking about it, replaying how I looked, wondering if other people noticed even though they keep saying they don’t see anything.\n\nIt’s upsetting because I know, logically, people tell me I look normal, and I try to believe them, but it still feels really obvious to me. It makes work harder, especially with all the video calls, and with salsa photos it’s like something that used to be fun now has this whole stressful part attached to it. I’ll be enjoying myself and then suddenly think, “What if someone takes a picture from the wrong side?” or “What if my face looks swollen again?” So… I’d say it causes a pretty significant amount of distress. Not every second of the day, but enough that it’s affecting how I feel about work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you completely avoid being seen by others because of how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always completely, but there are definitely times I avoid situations where I know I’ll be seen, especially if there’s a camera involved.\n\nAt work, it’s been more like I delay being seen. I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked in the preview window. I’ll tell myself, “Just join, it’s fine,” but then I notice the left side of my face and start adjusting the light or my angle, and suddenly I’m a few minutes late. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck in that whole spiral before meetings or after seeing myself on camera, and my supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was really humiliating. I didn’t explain the real reason in detail because it sounded… I don’t know, hard to justify.\n\nSocially, yes, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people would probably be taking photos. If someone posts pictures afterward, I’ll worry about seeing my face from the wrong angle, so sometimes I just don’t go. I’ll make excuses like I’m tired or busy, but really it’s because I don’t want to be seen like that or end up in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided intimate relationships because of how you think you look?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve avoided intimate relationships specifically in the same way, but… the bigger issue has been avoiding being seen, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s affected work and social stuff more clearly. I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past the way I looked in the camera preview, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck checking or fixing how my face looked. My supervisor even brought it up in a performance review, which was really awful and embarrassing.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people would probably be taking photos. I’ll tell people I’m busy or tired, but really it’s because I’m worried about being seen from a bad angle or ending up in pictures. So with intimacy… I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’ve avoided a relationship exactly, but I definitely avoid situations where I feel exposed or like someone might really look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to repeat over and over?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them urges exactly, but it feels like I have to keep doing these little checks and fixes, over and over, especially before meetings or going out.\n\nBefore a video call, I’ll open the camera preview and just… stare at it, basically. I’ll move my laptop, change the angle, turn a lamp on and off, pull my hair forward around the side of my face I’m worried about, then push it back because I think that looks obvious, then try again. I’ll tell myself I’m just making sure I look presentable, but it goes way past that. I’ll close the preview and then reopen it because I’m not convinced, or because I think maybe the lighting changed or I didn’t check from the right angle.\n\nIf I’m going out, it’s similar. I’ll retake pictures or look at myself in the phone camera, trying to see if my face looks strange or uneven. And sometimes I’ll ask friends, like, “Does my face look weird here?” or “Do I look swollen on this side?” And they’ll say no, but I don’t really feel reassured for long, so I’ll ask again in a slightly different way, which I know is probably annoying.\n\nIt can make me late, or by the time I’m done I’m so drained and embarrassed that I don’t even want to go anymore. It’s like I’m trying to get certainty, but I never actually get it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to form or maintain close relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though I guess I notice it more in the way I pull back from people than in one big dramatic relationship ending.\n\nLike with friends, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events because I knew people would probably be taking photos, and then I feel awkward afterward because people ask where I was or stop inviting me as much. I’ll say I was tired or had work, but the real reason is I couldn’t deal with the idea of my face being seen from a bad angle or posted somewhere. So then I’m not spending time with people, and it makes it harder to stay close.\n\nAnd even at work, which isn’t exactly “close relationships,” it affects how people see me. I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the screen, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing, and I think it made me seem unreliable. I hate that, because I do care about my work and the people I work with.\n\nSo… yes, it affects relationships, mostly by making me avoid things where I might be seen. Then people don’t really know the real reason, and I end up feeling more distant and ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern mainly about the left side of your face or do you worry about your appearance in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the left side of my face, yeah. I mean, I can worry about my appearance in general sometimes, but the thing I get stuck on—the thing that brought me in—is how uneven and puffy the left side looks to me, especially on video calls or in photos from salsa nights.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, but I’ll look at myself on camera and it’s like that side just jumps out at me. I start thinking it looks swollen or distorted, and then I can’t really focus on anything else. And with photos, especially ones other people take, I’m scared it’ll show from some awful angle and I won’t be able to unsee it.\n\nSo… not my whole appearance equally. It’s mostly that left side of my face, and how it looks uneven or puffy, and it’s gotten to the point where it’s interfering with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the left side of your face that you're trying to fix or hide when you're checking yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think what I’m trying to fix or hide is that the left side looks uneven to me, like it’s puffier or somehow heavier than the other side. Especially on video calls, because I’m just sitting there staring at my own face in that little box, and it feels like that side of my face is the only thing I can see.\n\nAnd in salsa photos too—those are really hard because I don’t control the angle or lighting, so I worry it’ll catch that side in the worst possible way. I’ll try to turn my face slightly, or pull my hair forward, or adjust the camera so that side doesn’t look so obvious. But then I worry the hiding itself looks obvious, so I keep checking again.\n\nEveryone tells me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more confused, because to me it feels really noticeable. That’s kind of why I came in—because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to interfere with work and going out and being around people.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance limited to the left side of your face or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly limited to the left side of my face. I mean, I can have normal insecurities like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole body or even my appearance in general in the same way.\n\nThe thing I came in for is that I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks to me, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. It’s like once I notice it, I just keep looking at it, and then everything else kind of fades into the background. And it feels strange saying that because people keep telling me they don’t see anything, or that it looks fine, but to me it looks really obvious.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I’m equally worried about other parts of my body. It’s really that left side of my face, and how it shows up on camera or in photos, and it’s gotten to the point where it’s messing with work and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Has your preoccupation with the left side of your face ever led to significant distress or impairment in your daily life, such as interfering with your work, social relationships, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I wish I could say it’s just something I notice and then move on from, but it’s become… it’s become a real problem.\n\nAt work, especially since I’m on video calls a lot, I’ve actually joined meetings late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview. I’ll adjust the lighting, move the laptop, change angles, turn my face a little, check again, and then suddenly I’m already late. And it’s embarrassing because from the outside it probably just looks like I’m being careless or disorganized, but I’m not trying to be late. I just get stuck.\n\nI’ve also missed ticket deadlines because I’ll lose time before or after calls looking at my face or thinking about how I looked during the call, and then I’m behind on actual work. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was awful. Not the face thing specifically—I didn’t really explain all of that—but the lateness and missed deadlines were mentioned, and I felt really ashamed because I knew what was causing it.\n\nSocially it’s affected me too. I’ve skipped salsa nights, which used to be something I really enjoyed, because I knew people would be taking photos or posting pictures afterward. And I’ve avoided some community events for the same reason. If I know cameras are going to be around, I start thinking about how the left side of my face is going to look, and sometimes it feels easier just not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fear that others are judging you or evaluating your appearance, especially when it comes to the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, that’s a big part of why it’s gotten so hard to deal with. It’s not just that I see the left side of my face as uneven or puffy when I’m looking at myself—although that’s definitely where it starts. It’s that once I notice it, I start imagining that other people must be noticing it too, especially if I’m on a video call or if someone is taking photos at salsa.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see anything, I don’t really feel reassured for very long. I’ll think, okay, maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they don’t want to make me feel bad. On video calls, I’m sitting there trying to listen, but part of my mind is stuck on that little image of myself and wondering if coworkers are seeing what I’m seeing. And with salsa photos, it’s almost worse because I don’t control the angle, and I worry that if the left side looks obvious in a picture, people will judge it or think I look strange.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel evaluated, especially around that part of my face. I came in because I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy it looks to me, even though everyone keeps saying they don’t notice it, and it’s starting to interfere with work and social stuff more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of this specific issue with the left side of your face, or are you more worried about being judged in general for how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s much more about that specific issue, the left side of my face. I mean, I can feel self-conscious like anyone else sometimes, but this doesn’t feel like a general “I don’t like how I look” thing.\n\nWhat I get stuck on is how uneven and kind of puffy the left side looks to me, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. That’s when I start thinking other people must be seeing that exact thing too, like they’re noticing that side of my face and judging it. It’s not usually that I think they’re judging my whole appearance in some broad way—it’s more like my attention just narrows down to that one part, and then I assume everyone else’s attention must be going there too.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people keep telling me they don’t see anything, or that I look normal, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I still end up focusing on it, and then it affects whether I can get through work calls or whether I want to go to salsa things where photos might be taken. So yes, it’s really tied to that specific left-side-of-my-face thing more than just being judged generally.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face something that you believe is actually there, or do you think it's just something you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I… I think it’s actually there, at least when I look at myself. That’s the hard part. I know people keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, but when I’m on a video call or I see a salsa photo, the left side of my face looks uneven and kind of puffy to me. It doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it out of nowhere.\n\nAt the same time, I guess part of me knows that maybe I’m focusing on it more than other people are. Because if everyone says they don’t notice it, then maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. But in the moment, when I see it, it feels very real. I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten to the point where it’s messing with work and social things. So… I don’t know if I’d say it’s “just a worry.” It feels like something I can see, even if other people say they can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your face being uneven or puffy might not be entirely accurate?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s a possibility. I don’t know how big of a possibility it feels like in the moment, if that makes sense. When I’m actually looking at myself on a video call, or I see a salsa photo where the angle catches the left side of my face, it feels very obvious to me—like the unevenness and puffiness are right there, and I can’t understand how someone wouldn’t notice it.\n\nBut then, outside of that moment, when I’m talking about it like this, I can admit that maybe my perception isn’t completely accurate. Because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see what I’m seeing, or that it’s not noticeable, and I guess it would be strange if literally no one else could see something that feels so glaring to me. So part of me knows I might be zeroing in on it in a way that makes it look worse to me than it really is.\n\nIt’s just… that doesn’t stop me from focusing on it. That’s why I came in, really. I can’t stop checking or noticing how uneven and puffy the left side looks to me, especially on work video calls and in photos from salsa, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life. So yes, I can say there’s a possibility I’m not seeing it accurately, but emotionally it still feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal to others, even if it doesn't to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s possible. I mean, I can say that sitting here, because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything wrong, or they’ll say I look normal, and logically I know that probably means it might look normal to other people.\n\nBut when I’m actually seeing myself, especially on video calls for work, it’s like my eyes go straight to the left side of my face. It looks uneven to me, kind of puffy, and then I start thinking, “How could nobody else be noticing this?” And the same thing happens with salsa photos. I’ll see a picture and I can barely look at the rest of it because I’m focused on that side of my face.\n\nSo yes, I guess it could look normal to other people even if it doesn’t look normal to me. That’s part of why I came in, honestly, because I can’t stop focusing on it even with people reassuring me, and it’s starting to affect work calls and whether I want to go out socially. It just doesn’t feel normal to me when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face might be different from how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it might be different. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to say that because when I’m looking at myself, it really doesn’t feel like “just my view.” It feels like I’m seeing something obvious—the left side of my face looks uneven, kind of puffy, especially on video calls or in those salsa photos. My attention just locks onto it.\n\nBut then I also know I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and that probably says something. Maybe I’m magnifying it somehow.\n\nIt’s just hard because even if part of me can admit that, it still affects me. So yes, I think my view could be different from reality, but in the moment it feels very real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being uneven or puffy is based on something you actually see, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on something I actually see, or at least I feel like I’m seeing it. It’s not just a vague feeling like, “Oh, I feel unattractive today.” It’s more specific than that. I’ll be on a work video call, and I’ll see the left side of my face on the screen, and it looks uneven to me, like puffier or heavier somehow, and then I can’t stop looking at it. If I know cameras are going to be around, I start thinking about how the left side of my face is going to look, and sometimes it feels easier just not to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fear that others are judging you or evaluating your appearance, especially when it comes to the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, that’s a big part of why it’s gotten so hard to deal with. It’s not just that I see the left side of my face as uneven or puffy when I’m looking at myself—although that’s definitely where it starts. It’s that once I notice it, I start imagining that other people must be noticing it too, especially if I’m on a video call or if someone is taking photos at salsa.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see anything, I don’t really feel reassured for very long. I’ll think, okay, maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they don’t want to make me feel bad. On video calls, I’m sitting there trying to listen, but part of my mind is stuck on that little image of myself and wondering if coworkers are seeing what I’m seeing. And with salsa photos, it’s almost worse because I don’t control the angle, and I worry that if the left side looks obvious in a picture, people will judge it or think I look strange.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do feel evaluated, especially around that part of my face. I came in because I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy it looks to me, even though everyone keeps saying they don’t notice it, and it’s starting to interfere with work and social stuff more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of this specific issue with the left side of your face, or are you more worried about being judged in general for how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s much more about that specific issue, the left side of my face. I mean, I can feel self-conscious like anyone else sometimes, but this doesn’t feel like a general “I don’t like how I look” thing.\n\nWhat I get stuck on is how uneven and kind of puffy the left side looks to me, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. That’s when I start thinking other people must be seeing that exact thing too, like they’re noticing that side of my face and judging it. It’s not usually that I think they’re judging my whole appearance in some broad way—it’s more like my attention just narrows down to that one part, and then I assume everyone else’s attention must be going there too.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people keep telling me they don’t see anything, or that I look normal, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I still end up focusing on it, and then it affects whether I can get through work calls or whether I want to go to salsa things where photos might be taken. So yes, it’s really tied to that specific left-side-of-my-face thing more than just being judged generally.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face something that you believe is actually there, or do you think it's just something you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I… I think it’s actually there, at least when I look at myself. That’s the hard part. I know people keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, but when I’m on a video call or I see a salsa photo, the left side of my face looks uneven and kind of puffy to me. It doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it out of nowhere.\n\nAt the same time, I guess part of me knows that maybe I’m focusing on it more than other people are. Because if everyone says they don’t notice it, then maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. But in the moment, when I see it, it feels very real. I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s gotten to the point where it’s messing with work and social things. So… I don’t know if I’d say it’s “just a worry.” It feels like something I can see, even if other people say they can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your face being uneven or puffy might not be entirely accurate?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s a possibility. I don’t know how big of a possibility it feels like in the moment, if that makes sense. When I’m actually looking at myself on a video call, or I see a salsa photo where the angle catches the left side of my face, it feels very obvious to me—like the unevenness and puffiness are right there, and I can’t understand how someone wouldn’t notice it.\n\nBut then, outside of that moment, when I’m talking about it like this, I can admit that maybe my perception isn’t completely accurate. Because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see what I’m seeing, or that it’s not noticeable, and I guess it would be strange if literally no one else could see something that feels so glaring to me. So part of me knows I might be zeroing in on it in a way that makes it look worse to me than it really is.\n\nIt’s just… that doesn’t stop me from focusing on it. That’s why I came in, really. I can’t stop checking or noticing how uneven and puffy the left side looks to me, especially on work video calls and in photos from salsa, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life. So yes, I can say there’s a possibility I’m not seeing it accurately, but emotionally it still feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal to others, even if it doesn't to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s possible. I mean, I can say that sitting here, because everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything wrong, or they’ll say I look normal, and logically I know that probably means it might look normal to other people.\n\nBut when I’m actually seeing myself, especially on video calls for work, it’s like my eyes go straight to the left side of my face. It looks uneven to me, kind of puffy, and then I start thinking, “How could nobody else be noticing this?” And the same thing happens with salsa photos. I’ll see a picture and I can barely look at the rest of it because I’m focused on that side of my face.\n\nSo yes, I guess it could look normal to other people even if it doesn’t look normal to me. That’s part of why I came in, honestly, because I can’t stop focusing on it even with people reassuring me, and it’s starting to affect work calls and whether I want to go out socially. It just doesn’t feel normal to me when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your view of your face might be different from how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it might be different. I mean, I don’t know exactly how to say that because when I’m looking at myself, it really doesn’t feel like “just my view.” It feels like I’m seeing something obvious—the left side of my face looks uneven, kind of puffy, especially on video calls or in those salsa photos. My attention just locks onto it.\n\nBut then I also know I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and that probably says something. Everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, or that it looks fine, and I keep thinking, okay, maybe the way I’m seeing it is not the same as how it actually looks to other people. Maybe I’m magnifying it somehow.\n\nIt’s just hard because even if part of me can admit that, it still affects me. On work video calls I’m distracted by it, and socially, with photos from dancing or just being around people, I keep worrying about how it looks. So yes, I think my view could be different from reality, but in the moment it feels very real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being uneven or puffy is based on something you actually see, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on something I actually see, or at least I feel like I’m seeing it. It’s not just a vague feeling like, “Oh, I feel unattractive today.” It’s more specific than that. I’ll be on a work video call, and I’ll see the left side of my face on the screen, and it looks uneven to me, like puffier or heavier somehow, and then I can’t stop looking at it. Or I’ll see a photo from salsa and immediately my eyes go there, to that side of my face, and I start picking apart how it looks.\n\nBut at the same time, I know everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything. So that’s what makes it confusing. Because to me it really looks visible, but apparently other people aren’t seeing it, or at least they say they’re not. So maybe it starts with something I think I see, and then my mind just locks onto it and makes it feel bigger than it is.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks to me, especially on video calls and in salsa photos, and it’s starting to interfere with work and with my social life. Even if part of me knows it might not look that way to others, when I see it, it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors, such as excessive grooming or checking, in an attempt to alleviate your concerns about the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. I mean, I don’t think of it as a “ritual” when I’m doing it, but it probably is, because it’s this whole sequence I get stuck in.\n\nBefore a work meeting, especially if it’s on video, I’ll open the camera preview over and over. I’ll check how the left side of my face looks, then I’ll shift my laptop a little, change the angle, turn a lamp on or off, move closer to the window, then decide the lighting makes it look worse, then move again. And I’ll keep adjusting my hair around that side, like trying to cover it or soften it somehow, even if it probably just looks like I’m fussing with my hair.\n\nIt happens before going out too. If I’m meeting friends or going dancing, I’ll take pictures to see how it looks, but then I don’t trust the first picture, so I take another one from a different angle, then another. Sometimes I retake them so many times that I feel worse than when I started, because I’m just staring at that side of my face.\n\nAnd I do ask people. Mostly friends. I’ll ask, “Does my face look weird?” or “Does this side look puffy?” And even if they say no, I’ll ask again in a different way, like maybe they didn’t understand what I meant. I know it probably gets annoying. It’s embarrassing to admit that.\n\nThe worst part is that it makes me late. I’ll think I’m just checking quickly before a meeting or before leaving, but then twenty or thirty minutes can go by, and by the end I’m either rushing and upset, or I feel so drained that I don’t even want to go anymore."
